steps.
XVI. NA TIONAL AIDS CONTROL PROGRAMME (NACP)
Recommendations/Observations
16.5 The Committee is constrained to note that against a Plan provision of Rs.815.00 crores
which was increased to Rs.953.89 crores at the RE stage during 2007-08 for NACP, expenditure
reported upto 13th March, 2008 was only Rs.767.34 crores. The Committee observes that optimum
utilization has not been achieved in any of the components of NACO. The Committee notes that
the Department has not been able to achieve optimum utilization of funds during 2006-07 also. The
Committee was given to understand that procedural delays in the procurement of equipment and
non-utilization of funds placed at the disposal of other Ministries like Ministry of Social Justice and
Empowerment and Information and Broadcasting were the main factors for under-utilization of
allocated funds during 2006-07. The Committee is afraid that similar reasons would be given
for 2007-08 also. It seems that weekly monitoring meetings at NACO level and quarterly
monitoring meetings with the State SACs followed by visits of teams of officers to the States to
ascertain the progress of work and level of expenditure have failed to make any noticeable
improvement. In view of enhanced allocation of Rs.1100.00 crores made for 2008-09, the
Committee recommends to the Department to take immediate corrective measures and make every
conceivable effort so as to ensure optimum and judicious utilization of funds.
Action Taken
The National AIDS Control Programme has utilized more than 96% of the allocated
funds since 2005-06. In the year 2005-06, the expenditure was Rs.532.7 crore against an RE of
Rs.533.5 crore (99.85%). Subsequently in the year 2006-07, the utilization came down to 96.7%
due to the non-utilization of funds at the disposal of MOSJE, I&B as well as the delays in
procurement process for a few items.
The amount allocated for 2007-08 was Rs.815.00 crores. In October, 2007 a request for
an enhanced allocation of Rs.977.8 crore was made. This additional amount was for
accommodating the Global Fund grants of Rs.139.00 crore under Round-VI. Despite constant
reminders, the revised allocation of Rs.954.00 crore was made only in the month of February-
March, 2008 which gave us little time to take up the implementation of some activities. However,
despite this constraint, NACO was able to incur an expenditure of Rs.926.21 crore against a FE
of Rs.943.34 crore accounting to 98.18 per cent. During the current financial year Rs.1100.00
crore has been allocated. Steps are being taken to ensure full utilization.

55
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE
The Committee is given to understand that steps are being taken to ensure full
utilization of funds during 2008-09. Y et no specific steps have been stated. In the opinion
of the Committee merely stating that steps are being taken to ensure full utilization
without specifying the steps being taken/to be taken is not adequate. The Committee,
therefore, would like to be apprised of the concrete steps being taken/contemplated to be
taken to obviate the possibility of under-utilization of funds sanctioned by Parliament.
Recommendations/Observations
16.13 From the information available with the Committee, it notes that services of as many as 177
NGOs have been discontinued. Besides that 48 Community Care Centres being run by NGOs have
been discontinued after on site evaluation. The Committee is dismayed at the fact that an alarming
percentage of NGOs were blacklisted and had to be removed. The Committee is not inclined to
admit that the whole problem was at the district level of officials. The Committee opines that either
this is a case of poor monitoring mechanism at the Department level or there is more to the matter
than what is visible. The Committee would like to have a Status Note on the performance of
NGOs, unspent balances available with the blacklisted NGOs and action taken for the recovery of
the same. Details of all similar cases/discrepancies related to NACO and the action taken by the
Department/CBI in that regard may also be furnished to the Committee.
Action Taken
The World Bank had conducted a Detailed Implementation Review (DIR) in 2006-07 for the
period 1999-2006 for the bank funded health sector projects, including the National AIDS Control
Programme Phase II. The bank had raised issues related to the selection of NGOs in Maharashtra,
Karnataka, Orissa and Chhattisgarh, inadequate monitoring and possibility of bribery and instances
where equipment for blood banks and diagnostic kits were not as per laid down guidelines. Many
of the allegations were anecdotal based on ‘confidential information’. The DIR report did not have
evidence or relevant material on individual persons who could be held responsible for malafide
reasons. The shortcomings noticed were systemic in nature. NACO took a detailed review and
senior officers visited the States. As there appeared to be some evidence of corruption by the then
Project Directors of Chhattisgarh and Karnataka, the matter has been referred to the CBI.
Many of the shortcomings mentioned in the DIR had already been addressed in NACP Phase
III and revised guidelines prepared in consultation with national experts, World Bank and other
donors. It is agreed that there were shortcomings in procurement in so far as the World Bank
procedures were concerned and action was initiated in consultation with World Bank officials.
These steps were taken to strengthen existing systems to ensure transparency and equity and
minimize potential areas of fraud and corruption. These activities were started well before the DIR
report.
Development of detailed guidelines and operational manuals for procurement of
NGOs and Public Private Partnerships: With the aim of improving, strengthening and
standardizing the quality of interventions, NACO developed detailed technical guidelines on
procurement of NGOs/CBOs and Public Private Partnerships. The guidelines outline key procedures
in standards to be maintained, financial procedures, procurement of stock and inventory, budgeting,
reporting, legal and statutory matters. In addition, guidelines on programme management for various
vulnerable groups, drug procurement, STI and ICTC linkages have also been developed. With
regard to procurement of NGOs for running TIs, SACS/NACO follow stringent procedures in
advertising for, short-listing, selecting and contracting NGOs. Call for EOIs are given out on

56
national/regional newspapers, applications are screened in desk reviews, field appraisals by the Joint
Appraisal Team (JAT), short listing by Technical Advisory Committee (TAC) with participation of
committees consisting of experts from donor agencies and the Government.
Introduction of evaluation of NGO performance by Committee having external
evaluator: During the launch phase of NACP-III, 958 were evaluated in April-June 2007 and 176
were discontinued.
301 TIs were terminated before the evaluation by different SACS/DACS due to various
reasons like poor-performance, inadequate focus on core target population, irregularities in financial
management etc.
By April, 2008, 742 targeted interventions were evaluated by an evaluation team consisting
of one representative from SACS, one external evaluator and one financial expert – such teams
were formed in each State and the process of evaluation lasted over one month and 21 TIs were
discontinued. The teams were trained by NACO and the evaluation tool was approved and
developed with support from the World Bank. The external evaluators were mainly development
consultants with in depth experience and knowledge of targeted interventions and HIV prevention
strategies. The external evaluators and SACS representatives provided detailed recommendation-
based reports to NACO highlighting the strengths and weaknesses of TIs in terms of organisational
capacity, human resources, programme delivery and financial systems. All TIs have also undergone
a voucher audit. The process is likely to be completed by July end.
Termination of contracts of about 25% NGOs
(i) 
Targetted Interventions -  With the conduction of evaluation and assessment of 952 TIs, the
quality of interventions was gauged by means of a standardised evaluation tool. 25% of the
evaluated TIs were found to be lacking in programme and financial management areas and were
discontinued as a result. The 952 TIs underwent the World Bank mandated “Annual Evaluation” in
order to ascertain which TIs could be continued under NACP III. The main reasons for
discontinuation was poor performance of the TIs under the evaluation criteria, Out of 952 TIs, 789
TIs passed the evaluation in 2007. During the process of evaluation, the NACO TI team assisted
the SACS in ‘reconfiguring’ TIs which would be continued - by ensuring that ‘non-core composite
TIs’ did not continue (covering both core and non-core populations, since this is against NACP III
guidelines), and by ensuring that any core populations covered under these non-core composites
were reallocated to other core TIs, or that they continued with a core target population focus.
(ii) 
Community Care Centres -  The Community Care Centres (CCC) were set up in phase I and
II as hospices to take care of the terminally ill patients and for the treatment of opportunistic
infections. Under Phase III, the role of the CCCs has been revised. These are now linked to the
Anti-retroviral therapy (ART) centres for adherence counselling and in patient care in the first week
of starting ART. CCCs will provide treatment of opportunistic infections, home based care and
follow up of patients who do not report back to the ART centre on due date. Funding to the CCCs
has been enhanced to enable them to meet the new responsibilities. In the year 2006-07, NACO
organised the first Appraisal for all existing CCC, which was conducted by ORG-Neilson. In the
Appraisal Report, 101 were found functional. Grading system was designed under the Appraisal in
an objective manner based on various parameters like physical infrastructure, systems, human
resources, services and monitoring. As per this assessment, 40 CCC were graded as ‘Very Good’
(A Grade), 56 CCC were performing ‘Average’ (B category) and 5 CCC were graded as ‘Poor’
(C category). All ‘A’ category CCCs are being continued support under NACP-III and for ‘C’
category CCCs support has been withdrawn. The ‘B’ categories CCC were informed of the existing
lacunae in their functioning to facilitate improvement in performance. They were continued support
only for recurring costs as per the sanction of NACP II, and were asked to reapply and compete

57
for being reselected. The selection was done as per the revised guidelines for CCC under NACP III
and initiated by open advertisement. Apart from the ORG Appraisal, NACO also conducted an
independent assessment of those NGOs which were not reported under the first Appraisal and
28 CCC which were categorised as Bl. As a result of both these appraisals, currently 78 CCC
(40+38) are functional as of Feb. 2008. No financial support was extended for C category CCCs that
were poor performing and closed. It is aimed to establish 350 CCCs during NACP Phase III.
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE

towards the Committee. The Committee takes this seriously and advises the Department to

XXI. MEDICAL EDUCATION
Recommendations/Observations
21.6 On being asked as to whether any mechanism was in place to check this, the Secretary,
Department of Health and Family Welfare stated that at present there is no mechanism to prevent
Indian students from going abroad to study. The Committee is of the view that the Department
needs to devise an effective mechanism, which could include legislation, so that Indian medical
students can be prevented from going to such places whose educational system does not match
with ours. The Committee would like to be informed about the steps taken by the Department in
this regard.
Action Taken
The observations of the Committee will be kept in view, while finalizing any mechanism to
prevent Indian students from going abroad to study.
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE
The Committee observes that the issue at hand is too urgent to be allowed to linger on,
as it concerns the quality and standards of medical education and any compromise in the matter
has the potential to put the lives of the people in peril. Therefore, mere assurance of keeping in
view the observations of the Committee at the time of finalizing any mechanism does not suffice.
In view of the gravity of the issue, the Committee would recommend to the Department to deal
with it with a sense of promptitude and bring some sort of policy or legislation urgently.

59
Recommendations/Observations
21.7 The Committee is anguished to note that the fate of so many students of the remaining
18 medical colleges, who have already appeared in their final examination, remains undecided. The
Committee is of the view that the students should not suffer due to such technicalities and
bureaucratic hurdles. A decision without any further delay may be taken in the matter. A Status
Note on such colleges may also be submitted to the Committee.
Action Taken
There are 27 medical colleges where the first batch of MBBS students has appeared for
final examination but are yet to be recognized. Out of these 27 medical colleges, Medical Council
of India (MCI) has sent its recommendations to the Central Government for recognition of MBBS
degree in respect of the 9 medical colleges. The status of remaining 18 medical colleges is at
Annexure-XII.
As per Section 11(2) of IMC Act, 1956, any university or medical institution in India which
grants a medical qualification not included in the First Schedule may apply to Central Government
to have such qualification recognized, and the Central Government, after consulting the Council,
may, by notification in the Official Gazette, amend the First Schedule so as to include such
qualification therein, and any such Notification may also direct that an entry shall be made in the
last column of the First Schedule against such medical qualification declaring that it shall be a
recognized medical qualification only when granted after a specified date.
The Council before recommending recognition of the degree conducts inspection of the
medical college/institution at the time of examination for the purpose of recommending to the
Central Government recognition of medical qualification granted by the university or medical
institution. The MCI verifies through inspection availability of staff, equipment, accommodation,
training infrastructure and other facilities as is required under the provisions of IMC Act, 1956 and
regulations made thereunder.
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE
Last year, Committee’s attention had been drawn to a very disturbing situation
whereunder as many as 27 medical colleges continued to function in the country whose first
batch of MBBS students had appeared for final examination but they were yet to be
recognized. The Committee had impressed upon the Department to resolve this matter
involving the fate of our students without any further delay. The Committee is deeply
disturbed to note that the position remains unchanged. No action seems to have been taken
in the case of 9 medical colleges where recommendations of MCI have reached the
Department. With respect to remaining 18 medical colleges, recommendations of MCI are
still awaited. The Committee is well aware of the statutory provisions regarding grant of
recognition to a medical college. The Committee views with serious concern this state of
affairs, that too in the critical area of medical education. The Committee is of the firm
opinion that this entire matter along with the existing procedure of recognition of medical
colleges needs to be reviewed by all concerned. The Department has to take a pro-active
role being the nodal authority for medical education in the country.

CHAPTER-I
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERV ATIONS WHICH
HA VE BEEN ACCEPTED BY THE GOVERNMENT
III. INFRASTRUCTURE STRENGTHENING
Recommendations/Observations
3.1 NRHM aims at strengthening of Primary Health Infrastructure and improving effective
service delivery across the country. Sub-centres, Primary Healthcare Centres and Community
Health Centres are the three pillars on which rural infrastructure is built. As on March, 2007, there
are 1,45,272 Sub-Centres, 22,370 PHCs and 4045 CHCs in the country. There is a shortfall of
20,855 Sub-Centres, 6491 PHCs and 2525 CHCs in the country based on the 2001 population
norms. The Committee has been constantly drawing the attention of the Department to the uneven
spread of these centres across the country with prominent gaps in the High Focus States. The
Committee finds that there is no marked improvement in this regard.
The Committee has been expressing its serious concern on the continuance of lack of rural
health infrastructure, especially in the High Focus States where the need is the most. On being
asked to identify the problem areas, the Committee has been informed that health infrastructure and
allocation of health workers under NRHM is planned on the population, work load and distance
norms. Shortages on the basis of 2001 population are quantified as the difference between the
required number of facilities/providers as per the norms and those that are functioning or in
position. This method of determining norms and shortage does not take into account the varying
epidemiological, geographical and socio-demographic patterns across the different States/
communities and between rural and urban areas within States. The actual availability of different
types of providers or the extent of private/voluntary sector involvement in healthcare delivery at
the community level is also not taken into account. The Committee notes that against the backdrop
of these very visible deficiencies, norms for setting up of the Sub-Centres and PHCs have been
modified by the Steering Committee of the Planning Commission. These norms to be adopted
during the Eleventh Plan have been duly approved by the Planning Commission also. The
Committee has been given to understand that population-centric norms are replaced with flexible
norms comprising habitation-based needs, community based needs and disease-pattern based needs.
Action Taken
The core objective of the National Rural Health Mission (NRHM) is to strengthen primary
healthcare infrastructure for effective service delivery especially in the rural areas. Acknowledging
the limitation of a population centric norm, NRHM has provided for construction of health facilities
based on caseload, distance and other criterions. Construction of Sub-Centres, PHC, CHC etc. have
been taken up from NRHM funds as well as Finance Commission funds released to States. The
suggestion of the Planning Commission has also been taken cognizance of by the Ministry.
V. NATIONAL T.B. CONTROL PROGRAMME
Recommendations/Observations
5.4 The Committee appreciates the successful implementation of RNTCP Phase-I and ongoing
Phase-II. Several initiatives/interventions taken at different levels and variety of implementing
64

65
agencies have contributed to the increasing population coverage and 85% cure rate. However, India
still contributes 20% of TB cases reported in the world. Out of 8.8 million cases per annum, 1.8
million cases are from India alone. New complex offshoots of TB also keep on emerging, the most
visible being worsening HIV epidemic increasing the number of TB cases. The Committee notes
that HIV is the most potent risk factor for progression of TB infection to disease form HIV-TB
patients detected in 2005 were 12,062, 13,614 in 2006 and 18,559 in 2007. The Committee is
aware that increase in number of co-infected (HIV–TB) patients being detected over the last three
years is a result of innovative and coordinated measures taken by the Department. But the other
dimension of this trend is increasing complexities involved in the treatment of such cases. The
Committee hopes that the National Framework formulated by RNTCP and NACP for joint TB/HIV
collaborative activities will lead to better and effective control of this emerging problem area.
Action Taken
With increasing number of HIV infected TB patients being routinely detected by the
cross-referral mechanisms between RNTCP and NACP, the programmes have prioritized providing
access to HIV care to the co-infected patients.
The Central TB Division, in collaboration with NACO, has successfully piloted a mechanism
for decentralized delivery of CPT to HIV infected TB patients through RNTCP and referral of all
such cases to nearest NACP ART Centre, where these patients would be promptly provided
monthly supplies of ART. These interventions have been included in the National framework for
joint TB/HIV collaborative activities and would be implemented in the country in a phased manner.
Recommendations/Observations
5.5 The Committee observes that the other major challenge to TB control in the country is
increasing number of MDR-TB cases. The Committee is aware that such cases are bound to
increase the magnitude and severity of TB epidemic and pose a major threat to TB control. The
Committee in its earlier Report has pointed out that 7% defaulters in a year is quite a significant
number of cases remaining untreated or half-treated. With a single TB patient infecting 10-15
persons in a year, rate of infection is bound to increase. The Committee would like to point out
that formulation of guidelines for management of MDR-TB cases and publication thereof will not
prove to be sufficient. The Committee had given a simple suggestion for identifying the defaulter
TB patients from the records being maintained at centres/hospitals with the help of NGOs/Social
Workers and Private Practitioners. Intention of the Committee was to identify such cases at the
first default stage itself. The Committee finds that under a new initiative a total of 50 MDR-TB
patients were to be registered for treatment in Gujarat and Maharashtra during 2007-08 and to be
doubled every year subsequently and ultimately in the entire country. While welcoming this
initiative, the Committee would also like to point out that cost of MDR-TB treatment is 300 times
more than non-MDR TB. The Committee can only say that preventive measures will be both cost-
effective and helpful for the patients. The Committee is of the firm opinion that through effective
IEC activities and alertness of health workers, regular intake of medicines can be easily monitored.
The Committee, therefore, suggests that in monitoring of TB patients the most vital aspect should
not be ignored at any cost.
Action Taken
The Ministry agrees with the observation of the Committee that measures to prevent the
development of MDR-TB will be both cost-effective and helpful for the patients. In consonance
with this spirit, the key focus of the programme is to make sure that the patients complete their
treatment as per schedule and on default the retrieval actions are taken immediately. Thus emphasis

66
of the programme is to implement quality DOTS to achieve highest cure rates and prevent MDR
TB.
The programme is laying lot of stress on ensuring provision of anti-TB drugs under the
direct observation. This aspect is covered during the modular training as well as reinforced during
supervisory visits and also during internal evaluations.
The RNTCP views management of MDR TB as a ‘standard of care’ issue and has identified
the following challenges and is addressing them on a priority basis:
• Minimizing default from treatment.
• Ensuring accurate categorization of previously treated patients as Category-II.
• Ensuring reliable DOT throughout treatment.
• Improve re-treatment success through intensified support, supervision, and monitoring
of DOT in category II patients through involvement of NGOs, PPs and Community
V olunteers.
• Improving Public-Private Mix (PPM) activities and uptake of DOTS by private sector
and medical colleges.
The Ministry agrees with the views of the Committee and focuses its highest priority to
reduce the default among TB patients through constant monitoring, involvement of NGOs, other
sectors and creating awareness through IEC activities.

by these States.

2. Appointment of the following Manpower:

focused attention on NE States and also in the States of Bihar, Jharkhand, J&K, Himachal Pradesh
and Uttarakhand would surely go a long way in paving way for accessible eye-care for the people
in those regions where presently there is dearth of proper eye-care facilities.
Action Taken
Matter of Record.
Recommendations/Observations
7.5 As per the existing scheme under the Programme, the NGOs perform cataract operations
directly under the supervision of Respective District Blindness Control Societies, which function
under the State Blindness Control Society at the State level. To a query regarding the monitoring
of the working of 881 NGO hospitals with eye-care facilities spread over in different parts of the
country, the Committee has been informed that working of NGO hospitals with eye care facilities
participating under NPCB are being monitored by the respective District Blindness Control Societies
directly. However, during State level meetings organized under the programme, NGOs’ hospitals are
also invited and the work done by them is reviewed. In addition, general guidelines for pre-
operative, operative and post-operative precautions for eye surgery have been circulated to all
States from time to time and last time these were circulated in the year 2007-08 to avoid eye
mishaps.
The Committee has been taking note of instances of malfunctioning of Eye Camps reported
in the Media from across the different States. The Committee feels that monitoring of Eye Camps
is being exclusively done by the District Blindness Control Societies. Department’s role starts as
and when reports regarding malfunctioning of Eye Camps held in States are brought to the notice
of the Ministry. Corrective measures are accordingly taken by the Ministry. The Committee,
however, feels that the Department needs to be more pro-active in such cases. One corrective
measure which can be initiated by the Department without any special efforts involved is to review

68
the performance of such Eye Camps in the State level meetings, with required follow-up action
taken on the reported incidents of malfunctioning of Eye Camps. The Committee is also of the
view that follow-up treatment after Eye Camps should be made mandatory and strictly enforced.
District hospital authorities also need to be involved in the conducting of such Eye Camps by
introduction of compulsory pre-checks in Eye Camps before start of Eye surgery and other
treatment. The Committee would like to add here that the Department should also devise some
method of surprise inspection of eye camps organized by the NGOs to facilitate cataract
operations. This, the Committee feels would ensure stricter compliance by all the concerned entities
and help reduce eye mishaps. The Committee notes that the process for independent evaluation of
NPCB including evaluation of work being done by NGOs has been initiated. The Committee would
appreciate if this exercise is expedited so that corrective measures are taken.
Action Taken
The National Programme for Control of Blindness (NPCB) has been circulating guidelines
on “Pre-operative, Operative and Post-Operative precautions for Eye Surgery” to States for further
dissemination to all State level hospitals, Medical Colleges, Regional Institutes of Ophthalmology and
NGO hospitals from time to time to avoid eye mishaps. As a follow up action, State Governments
are being directed separately to ensure that these guidelines are strictly followed in all eye care
facilities and Eye Screening Camps in their States. It has also been proposed to make it mandatory
for the State Governments to obtain an undertaking regarding strict compliance of the guidelines
from the organizers before granting permission to them for conducting Eye Screening Camps.
Now, Eye Screening Camps are allowed but the Eye operations in the camps have been banned
by Government of India due to high rate of mishaps in such camps w.e.f. 1997.
Recommendations/Observations
7.9 The Committee’s attention has also been drawn to the findings of a survey on Rapid
Assessment of TRACHOMA cases done in the district of Haryana, Gujarat, U.P., Uttarakhand,
Punjab and Rajasthan. Findings of this survey were that against the popular perception of there
being no transmission of trachoma cases in India, all districts showed active cases.
The Committee notes with disappointment that the target to bring prevalence of blindness to
0.8% by 2007 has not been reached. The Department has informed that as per the report of Rapid
Survey on Avoidable Blindness (2007), the prevalence of blindness has come down to 1% and that
Cataract remains the single largest cause of blindness, low vision and one eye blindness in India. The
Committee also infers from the above study that the prevalence of blindness due to Trachoma also
continues to be there. It is a well known fact that blindness is a bane of nature. The Committee
believes that most of the people in smaller towns and rural areas are not aware of the symptoms
of the above ailments, and the methods to manage eye care, hence their eye-care service seeking
behavior pattern do not change. The Committee exhorts the Department to take all possible necessary
steps to devise IEC activities in local media and television channels so that awareness among the
people is increased. The Committee believes a lot of cases of blindness can be checked by such a
measure and would in the long run help to achieve the targeted goals under the programme.
Action Taken
On the basis of findings of the Survey on Rapid Assessment of TRACHOMA about
presence of Trachoma cases in some pockets of some States in the country, IEC activities for
public awareness about Trachoma have been intensified through electronic media such as Radio,
Television, print and press media etc. The guidelines in this regard have also been issued to States
including NE States. Spots on TV and Radio have been produced in regional languages also.

9.11 The Committee notes that the number of JSY beneficiaries, which was only 5.73 lakhs during

This increasing number of beneficiaries can be considered a very positive sign, however, the

has to be made to ensure that the benefits of this Noble Scheme reach the targeted beneficiaries.

however notes that 25% of Rs.793 crores utilized under Mission Flexipool were used for other
activities under NRHM. The Committee would like to have an idea about the details of such activities.

Recommendations/Observations
9.12 The Committee, while welcoming this Scheme with an objective of reducing Maternal and
Neo-Natal Mortality by promoting institutional delivery, would like to draw the attention towards
some emerging problem areas being noticed in its implementation. Under the present set up, ASHA
is a worker who has been entrusted with a very large number of responsibilities with her role in
JSY being very crucial.
Reports are being received from various sources that there is a lot more to the
compensation received from under the programme than is visible to the naked eye. There are
instances when institutional deliveries are being falsely reported and the entire compensation
package is being misused.
Action Taken
The State/UT Governments have been advised to institute a two-tier monitoring system to
monitor the process of implementation of the scheme in the States and UTs as well as
disbursement of cash and record keeping of financial transactions. This has been done with a view
to reduce possibilities of situation as has been mentioned in the report. Further, with a view to
eliminate any chances of loopholes in the payment system, the State Governments have been
advised to ensure the distribution of cash assistance to the beneficiaries and ASHAs through
cheques, preferable by account payee ones. Further, guidelines on grievance redressal mechanism
have also been sent to the States/UT Governments for necessary action.
In addition, Joint Review Missions (JRM) comprising representatives of this Ministry and
the Development Partners, undertake visits to 5 or 6 States twice a year. In each State, the JRMs
make detail study of the functioning of the various interventions under NRHM, including JSY by
inspecting facilities ranging from Sub-Centres to District hospitals, in 2 to 3 districts.
Observations of the Committee have been noted and will be brought to the notice of the
States/UT Governments for further tightening of the disbursement system.

BUDGETARY ALLOCATION (NRHM)
Recommendations/Observations
2.7 Pendency status of Utilization Certificates in respect of the Department of Health and Family
Welfare is given below:
Annexure-B of Report
The pendency level of Utilization Certificates has been engaging the attention of the
Committee for the last few years. According to latest figures as on 1.02.2008, there were as many
as 1458 Utilization Certificates in respect of Health Sector amounting to Rs.2575.02 crores for the
grants released upto 2005-06. The Committee fails to understand the reasons for pendency of UCs
continuing for such a long period. As per records made available to the Committee, States like
Bihar, Chhattisgarh, Gujarat, M.P., Maharashtra, Orissa, Rajasthan, U.P. have the maximum number
of pending UCs. On a specific query regarding any plans of the Department to reach a justifiable
level of pendency, the Committee is given to understand that a communication was sent by the
Chief Controller of Accounts (CCA) to all the States through Resident Commissioners on this issue
giving full details of pending UCs in respect of the Department of Health and Family Welfare. The
Committee is dismayed to note that in the name of efforts, only a letter has been issued. The
Committee feels that spirited efforts, which are apparently lacking, need to be made on priority
basis to clear the UCs which are pending for the last three decades or so. The Committee also
recommends to the Department to take up the case with concerned State Governments at the
highest level so as to assess whether the funds have been utilized properly or not.
Action Taken
The pendency level of Utilization Certificates has come down to 1278 amounting to
Rs.2012.71 crores as on 1st May, 2008. During the year 2007-08, 499 Utilisation Certificates
amounting to Rs.1401.75 crores were liquidated. The year-wise details are at Annexure-III. The
Department is taking monthly review of outstanding Utilisation Certificates at the level of Health
Secretary besides issuing periodical reminders at the appropriate level for early clearance. In this
regard a DO was issued by Secretary (H&FW) to all Principal Secretaries of the States for
submission of pending Utilization Certificates on priority basis. Fresh grants were not released
during the year 2007-08 to the Grantees having outstanding Utilization Certificates against them.

III. INFRASTRUCTURE STRENGTHENING
Recommendations/Observations
3.2 The Committee welcomes the modification in the criteria for assessing the requirement of
health infrastructure in the country. In a vast country like ours, rigid norms based on population
only made uniformly applicable were perhaps not proving to be justified and not based on ground
realities. The Committee is also of the view that change in the norms for health infrastructure
proposed by the Planning Commission was called for. The Committee is, however, not aware
whether any preparatory exercise has been initiated for conducting surveys as per the revised
norms. The Committee is afraid that if this issue is allowed to linger on for long, the very objective
of NRHM of bridging gaps in the health care will continue to be a distant dream. The Committee,
accordingly, impresses upon the Department to initiate action at the earliest.
Action Taken
The National Programme Coordination Committee (NPCC), set up under NRHM, takes note
of the need based projection for construction of facilities as proposed by the States in their State
Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs) while evaluating their Plans. Approvals are based on the
extant and future capacity for manpower augmentation in the States so that the expansion does not
remain a brick and mortar exercise. Emphasis is on consolidation and optimal utilisation of existing
facilities and also alternative strategies like Mobile Medical Units, partnership with the non-

73
governmental sector to expand reach. In the approvals of NPCC in the year 2008-09, an amount
of Rs.80 crores to U.P., Rs.3 crores to Uttarakhand, Rs.7.5 crores to Jharkhand, have been
allocated for construction of new Sub-Centres. An amount of Rs.30 crores has been allocated for
construction of PHCs in Bihar, Rs.l4 crores in Jharkhand and Rs.18.25 crores for CHCs in
Jharkhand as few illustrative examples of the commitment of the Mission towards facility
expansion. Since the key human resource constraints will take some time to redress, the expansion
necessarily has to be a phased one.
Recommendations/Observations
3.5 NRHM proposes to upgrade all CHCs to IPHS standards by 2010 in a phased manner and
all States/UTs are required to complete the IPHS facility survey of the existing CHCs as a first step
towards the upgradation to IPHS level. The Department has presented before the Committee that
reports of IPHS Facility Survey for 2453 CHCs has been completed in 31 States/UTs while that
which are physically received is 957, while upgradation work has started in 1161 CHCs and
completed in 441 CHCs.
The Committee is happy to note that the situation in this regard has improved over the past
year’s figures but at the same time its attention is also drawn to the number of facility survey
reports received from the following states:—
States CHCs Facility surveys conducted
Uttar Pradesh 386 169
Madhya Pradesh 270 96
West Bengal 346 211
Maharashtra 407 145

The Committee is dismayed to note that facility surveys in States like Karnataka, Tamil Nadu,
Madhya Pradesh and Maharashtra have not been treated with the earnest it deserves. Except for West
Bengal and Andhra Pradesh, not even one state mentioned above has been able to reach the 50% mark
in furnishing the facility survey reports. What is more disturbing is the fact that not one report has
been received from Goa. Though the Department has given that the states are doing extremely well
in the progress in work, the Committee is constrained to note that the data cited above do not
support the above stand. It urges the Department to impress upon the State Governments the need
to focus their attention on submitting the survey reports on time and thereby meet the timelines for
their upgradation by 2010, the realization of which would otherwise remain a far cry.
Action Taken
Karnataka, Tamil Nadu and Maharashtra have been urged in the NPCC meetings to expedite
the process of Facility Survey and they have started taking corrective measures. As on date, IPHS

74
facility survey for 2569 CHCs has been completed in 31 States/UTs while upgradation work has
started in 1493 CHCs and completed in 483 CHCs. Upgradation of PHCs/CHCs have been taken
up in Karnataka, Maharashtra, Tamil Nadu and M.P. as well.
Recommendations/Observations
3.10 The Committee has been given to understand that an ANM has been entrusted with the
following responsibilities:—
• To assist in births at homes in rural areas,
• To locate pregnant women and take them for Ante Natal Check-ups and at least three
Post Natal Check-ups, to refer complicated pregnancy cases,
• To immunize the pregnant women and oversee the administration of folic acid pills etc.
• To immunize the new born and oversee the checkup and timely immunisation of the
infant,
• after the introduction of the NRHM, in some of the states she has also undergone
three weeks of training in contraception methods/techniques such as insertion of IUDs,
administration of oral contraceptive pills, counseling etc.
From the above information, the Committee understands that the ANM plays a very crucial
role in the rural areas because of her skill and training in safe deliveries and therefore her
contribution to the RCH programme is very vital. Her presence is even more important in those
states that have very high MMR and IMR. This presence of ANMs in Sub-Centres is essential
where proper access to institutional healthcare is not available.
Action Taken
Acknowledging the need to strengthen the Sub-Centre and the criticality of the ANM as
improved health indicator, NRHM has provision for a second ANM in the Sub-Centres. As on date,
more than 97% of the Sub-centres are functional in the States/UTs with at least one ANM (141178
SC out of 145272 SCs) and 22471 SCs have 2nd ANMs also. Sub-Centre construction is an
important Mission activity that has been taken on a large scale through the State PIPs. Efforts to
improve presence of ANMs at Sub-Centres have also been attempted by directing States to cancel
deputation of ANMs away from Sub-Centres.
RURAL HEALTH FUNCTIONARIES
Recommendations/Observations
3.15 ANMs/MPWs(M)/LHVs are the key functionaries of Sub-Centres and PHCs. Their presence
is essential for the smooth functioning of these centres. The Committee has been observing year
after year the existence of gaps between the required and existing strength in all the States. As on
March, 2007, 14,180 posts of ANMs, 29653 posts of MPWs(M) and 2497 LHVs are lying vacant
in various States. The Committee is unhappy to observe that there seems to be no noticeable
improvement. Details about some of the High Focus States as on March, 2007 are self-revealing:—

The Committee observes that the recommendations of Task Force like local preference,

The Committee finds that the appraisal of Common Review Mission conducted in November, 2007
has highlighted the shortage of manpower at all levels in Sub-Centres/PHCs/CHCs in Andhra

is followed by an action plan for making available the required manpower in these States. The
Department has to play a pro-active role without any further delay. One must not forget that the
very objective of NRHM of strengthening of primary health infrastructure rests on the availability
of key health functionaries at the right places.
Action Taken
Improving availability of critical manpower at health care institutions in the rural areas is one
of the key thrust areas under NRHM. The States/UTs were advised to recruit human resources based
on local criteria as per mandate of NRHM. Under NRHM, State/UT Governments are being funded
for contractual appointment of trained health personnel like doctors, ANM, S taff Nurse and other
Para-Medics etc. through their PIPs. States have taken several steps for ensuring availability of skilled
health personnel and we expect to move further in the right direction. As regards recruitment of
skilled health personnel, a total of 2282 Specialists, 6271 doctors, 12908 Staff Nurses and 4380 other
category para-medical staff had been added under NRHM on contract basis, upto December, 2007.
Since availability of human resources is a constraint, the process will take some time.

MALARIA
Recommendations/Observations
4.3 Committee’s attention has also been drawn by the following anticipated constraints/problem
areas pointed out by the Department in the context of Action Plan for 2008-09 for Malaria control.
– Filling up of vacant posts of Health Workers.
– Timely release of funds by States.
– Submission of UCs by States to GoI.
– Timely procurement of bed nets and insecticide for treatment.
The Committee strongly feels that mere chalking out an elaborate Action Plan and allocation
of funds for control of a major disease does not seem to make any sense if year after year same
constraints seem to persist. The Committee once again tries to impress upon the Department to
take immediate steps for removal of bottlenecks in the successful implementation of Malaria Control
Programme. The Committee would like to mention that under the National Vector Borne Disease
Control Programme, the major component is Malaria Control having the maximum allocation of
Rs.351.68 crore out of a provision of Rs.440.03 crores for the entire programme in 2008-09.
Therefore, it is in the fitness of things that concerted efforts are also made for optimum and
judicious utilisation of allocated funds.
Action Taken
The action plan has been discussed in detail with States and the Districts have been
prioritized for focused intervention towards control of malaria. The implementation activities,
judicious use of allocated funds and timely submission of SOEs/UCs have also been discussed in
meetings held under NRHM.
The high malaria endemic areas have been identified and GIS mapping for most of them
has been done for focused intervention for optimum and judicious utilization of resources.
Recommendations/Observations

the NE States and Assam, Andhra Pradesh, Oriss a and West Bengal for the year 2007 is given below.

The overall Pf % in the country has been around 50% since last decade, however, in some

Under the programme, each reported malaria death case is investigated and only those cases
are finally recorded as malaria death cases whose blood smear is prepared and found positive for
a sexual stage of P .falciparum parasite.

79
Recommendations/Observations
4.6 The Committee has been persistently drawing the attention of the Department to the urgent
need for implementation of all malaria control measures in Malaria endemic States of NE Region,
Orissa and West Bengal. The Committee is well aware that NE States together contribute 4% of
the country’s population, 12% of malaria cases, 18% of Pf cases and 54% of malaria deaths
reported in the country during 2006. Figures must have remained unchanged in 2007 also. Another
cause of concern is increasing trend of resistance to conventional anti-malarial drug Chloroquine
noticed in NE States. The Committee notes that Global Fund supported Intensified Malaria Control
Project in NE States being implemented since July, 2005 with a contribution of US $ 30 million
for the first two years of the project also seems to have failed to have the desired impact.
Action Taken
It is submitted that during the year 2007, though the NE States together constitute 4% of
country’s population only, 13% of malaria cases, 16.66% of Pf cases and 45.52% of malaria deaths
have been reported therefrom.
The approved Budget for Years I and II for Phase-I (July, 2005 to June, 2007)
was US $ 30 million. However, based on the expenditure, the Global Fund has obligated only
US $ 13.4 million during the Phase-I of the project. During March, 2007 the performance of Global
Fund supported intensified Malaria Control Project was rated as B1 by the Country Coordinating
Mechanism. Thereafter the project was approved for Phase-II funding by the Global Fund. The
accelerated implementation has resulted into further grant of US $ 50.12 million for Phase-II. In
Phase-II, technical manpower, like 150 malaria technical supervisors and 100 LTs, have been
allotted to these States for deploying in high malaria endemic areas. Considering the high incidence
of P .jalciparum malaria and chloroquine resistance in the various parts of the country including
project States like NE States, Jharkhand and Orissa, the policy decision has been taken to treat all
Pf cases with Artesunate + Sulpha pyremethamine (ACT) in 110 districts, chloroquine resistant foci
and surrounding cluster blocks of these focis which include 60 districts of NE States, 12 districts
of Jharkhand and 13 districts of Orissa.
KALA-AZAR
Recommendations/Observations
4.9 The Committee has been observing increasing trend of Kala-azar cases. In 2006, 39178
cases and 187 deaths were reported with 162 deaths occurring in Bihar alone. Position seems to
have worsened further in 2007. Upto November, 2007, already 35,638 cases with 158 deaths have
been reported. State-wise figures upto May, 2007 indicate that out of 79 deaths reported till then,
64 deaths have occurred in Bihar. While the Committee would not dispute the Department’s stand
that intensive surveillance has led to reporting of increased number of cases, the fact remains that
treatment compliance of Kala-azar cases detected is far from satisfactory in view of alarming rate
of deaths being reported. Number of initiatives for Kala-azar elimination undertaken seem to have
made no impact with ground reality continuing to be as discouraging as before. The Committee
understands that 200 villages of 9 districts reporting large number of Kala-azar cases have been
selected for focused interventions.
The Committee strongly feels that effective surveillance, timely treatment and compliance of
treatment regimens in all PRCs can perhaps lead to better reports in 2008. The Committee is of the
view that only if intensive monitoring at all levels is carried out, the goal of elimination of Kala-azar
by 2010 can be made achievable. Situation so far has not been very encouraging. All the quantifiable
objectives listed in the Action Plan for 2008-09 need to be closely monitored on monthly basis.

80
Action Taken
Case search was intensified and 44001 cases were detected and treated in 2007. Adequate
stock of anti-kala-azar drugs is available at the peripheral level. 96% of targeted population under
Kala-azar in 2007 was covered with DDT. The monitoring has been strengthened and in addition
the initiatives taken in 2007, the Kala-Azar regional and State coordinators have been placed for
strict supervision and monitoring. Central teams are visiting to the high endemic districts especially
during spraying so that the activities are geared up and quality and coverage are improved. Besides,
the situation is reviewed by GoI at highest level.

5.6 The Committee is disappointed to note that in spite of repeated cautioning by it regarding
the issue of unspent balances being carried forward to the next year there has been no
improvement in the situation. Despite various measures like training of Accountants, adherence to
financial norms through inspection of records and visits undertaken by the Department, the
expenditure figures in respect of many of the States reveal that huge amounts have been lying
unutilized. The Committee had in its 22nd Report emphasized the need for streamlining the
utilisation pattern of States/UTs. However, the situation has seen little change over the past year.
From the information provided by the Department to a specific query regarding allocation and
utilization of funds for the year 2007-08 a very gloomy picture is revealed. Many States as
indicated below do not have the capacity to make optimum utilisation:
Annexure-VI of Report
The Committee is apprehensive that the trend of under utilisation during this year must have
worsened even more. The Committee has been time and again emphasizing that availability of
unspent balances and under-utilisation of funds is tantamount to persisting flaws in implementing
the Programme. The Committee, at the risk of being repetitive, would impress upon the Department
that it should discuss the problems with the programme directors and evaluate as to why the
programme does not take off at the required tempo and what possible steps could be taken to bring
about better implementation of the programme.
Action Taken
The Ministry is convinced about the need to have certain minimum balances in the States
at the end of the financial year. Efforts have also been made to reduce the closing balances as this
adversely affects fresh releases. Therefore, the most appropriate time to see the closing balance
is 31st March of ending financial year. As shown in the Table below, unspent balances as on
31st March for the country as a whole are showing a definite downtrend for the last 4 years. The
unspent balance of Rs.15.91 crores at national level for 2008-09 calculated for each district is
about Rs.2.52 lakhs per district which is the bare minimum amount which is required to be
available with districts at the beginning of next financial year till such time regular funds are
released for the financial year. Even for the States under reference, the closing balances for the
last 4 years are showing a downtrend except for the State of Madhya Pradesh. Even for the State
of Madhya Pradesh the unspent amount per district works out to be mere Rs.5.09 lakhs which is
just sufficient enough to meet expenses till the time regular funds are made available considering
the population of State. The Table given below clearly demonstrates that through the consistent
efforts of the programme division, the closing balance as on 31st March has been brought down
to levels sufficient enough to sustain activities in next financial year till regular funds are made

Recommendations/Observations
5.9 Committee’s attention has also been drawn by Studies like Social Assessment Study,
Institutional Assessment Study in respect of TB Control measures undertaken so far. The Committee
would like to point out that TB infection is no more confined to the poor and marginalized sections
of the society. Growing number of patients now belong to middle class and also affluent population.
There is an urgent need for making an analysis of basic factors behind this emerging situation which
can ultimately reach to alarming proportions. The Committee hopes that with the availability of both
funds and manpower, the Department will take up such an exercise at the earliest.
Action Taken
Survey undertaken by TB Research Centre, Chennai (TRC) has shown that the prevalence
of TB is around 4 times higher amongst the lower socio-economic strata as compared to the
higher. However, TB being an airborne infection, can affect individuals from any of the socio-
economic groups. Studies indicate that some of the other key predisposing factors, besides
overcrowding and malnutrition, include smoking, diabetes, HIV co-infection etc. which cut across
all the socio-economic groups. The most effective way to achieve TB control is to cut the
transmission of infection by early diagnosis and prompt treatment of all TB patients. The
programme is hence focusing on providing quality assured diagnostic and treatment services which
are easily accessible to all the sections of the community. The programme has plans to repeat the
social assessment study during the current phase of RNTCP project wherein some of these factors
would be evaluated.
VI. NATIONAL LEPROSY ERADICATION PROGRAMME (NLEP)
Recommendations/Observations
6.1 National Leprosy Eradication Programme was launched with the objective of eliminating
Leprosy i.e. achieving prevalence rate of <1case/10,000 population in the country. National level

82
elimination of Leprosy has been achieved since 31 December, 2005 when the prevalence rate
came down to 0.95 or less than 1/10,000 population. 0.83 lakh cases were on record as on
1st April, 2007 giving a Prevalence rate (PR) of 0.72 per 10,000 population. 31 States/UTs have
achieved the level of elimination i.e. PR less than 1/10,000 population. 80% of districts and 75%
of blocks across the country have also achieved elimination. Four States, namely Bihar,
Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand and West Bengal having PR between 1 and 2 per 10,000 population with
Chhattisgarh having more than 2 per 10,000 population are yet to achieve elimination status.
Action Taken
Matter of Record.
Recommendations/Observations
6.2 The budget allocation was Rs.40.00 crores in 2007-08. As the States had unspent balances
with them, the BE 2007-08 was reduced to Rs.27.12 crores at RE stage. Expenditure reported upto
29.02.08 is only Rs.21.57 crore. Allocation of Rs.45.00 crore has been made for 2008-09. The
increased provision is meant for incentives to be given to leprosy affected persons undergoing re-
constructive surgery and institutions performing re-constructive surgery and performance based
incentive to ASHA in S tates with high leprosy burden such as Bihar, Uttar Pradesh, Jharkhand,
Chhattisgarh and West Bengal.
The Committee, in its 22nd Report, had voiced its serious concern for under-utilisation of
allocated funds in 2006-07 due to unspent balances in majority of the States. The Committee takes
note of detailed analysis of actual expenditure pattern in 2006-07 carried out by the Department.
The Committee is surprised to observe that even this detailed analysis indicates provisional figures
of 77.59% of utilisation of allocated funds by the States. The Committee’s concern is further
deepened by the fact that the two major States Bihar and Jharkhand could utilize only 33.9% and
30% of the allocated funds during the year 2006-07. Creation of confusion due to merger of the
leprosy society with the State Health Society under NRHM has been cited as the main reason for
this position. The Committee has been given to understand that a number of measures like review
of programme performance and expenditure pattern in the quarterly review meetings of district
Leprosy Officers, streamlining of procurement procedure and monitoring at the central level were
initiated during 2007-08. The Committee also notes that more focus was given on implementation
of Action Plan during annual meeting and quarterly review meetings of State Programme Officers.
Not only this the States were also instructed to prepare a month-wise Action Plan of IEC and
Training from July, 2007 onwards and submitting the monthly implementation reports of these
activities. The Committee is, however, constrained to observe that these elaborate measures initiated
by the Department failed to make any visible impact as the ground realities continue to remain
unchanged. Drastic reduction of allocated funds had to be made at RE stage in 2007-08 with even
that provision remaining under-utilised upto 29.02.08.
Action Taken
During 2007-08, the budget allocation was reduced from Rs.40.00 crores to Rs.27.12 crores
at RE stage. Reduction in RE stage was carried out due to huge unspent funds from previous year
lying with State Health Societies. This was to the tune of Rs.14.27 crores. Majority of unspent
balance was lying with the States of Bihar (Rs.213.79 lakhs), Jharkhand (Rs.62.89 lakhs), Madhya
Pradesh (Rs.118.60 lakhs), Uttar Pradesh (Rs.169.52 lakhs) and West Bengal (Rs.90.10 lakhs).
Expenditure under the programme during 2007-08 was Rs.25.01 crores. During 2007-08,
more focus was given on implementation of Action Plan during NLEP annual meetings and quarterly

83
review meetings conducted in various States. States were further instructed to prepare month-wise
Action Plan of IEC and Training during 2007-08 and submit monthly report of these activities. It
was due to these efforts that the pace of expenditure was increased by the States and the
Programme could utilize an amount of Rs.25.01 crores i.e. 92% of the RE.
Unspent funds lying with the States have also decreased to Rs.6.73 crores during 2007-08
as compared to Rs.14.27 crores during 2006-07.

Action Taken
Out of the total outlay of Rs.1928.60 crores under RCH Flexible Pool, the expenditure upto
29.2.2008 is stated as Rs.1643.62 crores which was 85%. Only Rs.284.98 crore (Rs.1928.60 crore–
Rs.1643.62 crore), i.e., only 15% remained to be spent during the last month of the financial year
2007-08. However, the total expenditure is shown as Rs.1842.6 crore. This means that an
expenditure of Rs.198.44 crore (Rs.1842.06 crore – Rs.1643.62 crore), i.e., only 10% of the total
outlay of Rs.1928.60 crore was made during the last month, i.e., March, 2008.

84
Similarly, under the Mission Flexible Pool, out of the total outlay of Rs.3155.32 crores, the
expenditure upto 29.2.2008 is stated as Rs.2505.05 crores which was 80%. Only Rs.650.27 crore
(Rs.3155.32 crore–Rs.2505.05 crore), i.e., only 21% remained to be spent during the last month
of the financial year 2007-08. However, the total expenditure was Rs.3151.63 crore. This means
that an expenditure of Rs.646.58 crore (Rs.3151.63 crore–Rs.2505.05 crore), i.e., only 20% of the
total outlay of Rs.3155.32 crore was made during the last month, i.e., March, 2008.
It appears from the above that the actual pace of utilization under the RCH Flexible Pool,
i.e., only 10% during the last month of the last quarter of 2007-08 was appropriate. However, there
was some variation under the Mission Flexible Pool which is owing to the fact that funds for
various activities are released from State to district level and from districts to various implementing
agencies all over the year. However, the reporting of expenditure on most of the items takes place
during the last quarter of the year as a result higher expenditure gets reflected during the last
quarter and the last month of the year, though it pertains to the earlier quarters. Therefore, owing
to the long channel of reporting process, higher expenditure gets reflected during the last quarter/
month ofthe financial year.
Recommendations/Observations
9.6 Under RCH Flexipool, major allocation (57%) is meant for Maternal Health followed by
infrastructure share of 12% and 13% for family planning measures. As per the Financial Monitoring
Report for the first three quarters of 2007-08, Rajasthan, Orissa, M.P., are the States performing
well so far as utilization of allocated funds is concerned. However, the position is different in
respect of the most crucial States like Bihar registering only 25% of utilisation, Jharkhand 30% and
Chhattisgarh 34%. Non-EAG States have been able to achieve utilization of more than 85% of their
allocated funds with Goa (9%) and Tamil Nadu (18%) being the low performing States. Reports
are satisfactory with regard to NE States of Arunachal Pradesh, Assam, Mizoram and Sikkim.
The Committee would like to point out that in view of increased demands from the States
for various activities under RCH Flexipool, allocation was enhanced to Rs.1928.58 crores at the
RE Stage from Rs.1725.00 crore provided in BE 2007-08. Availability of opening balance of
Rs.542.12 crore at the beginning of the year with additional increase at the RE Stage clearly
indicates that there is no shortage of funds. It seems that either the States do not have the capacity
to take the maximum benefit of different components of the programme or rigid procedural
formalities for release of funds are coming in the way. The Committee has been given to
understand that with regard to Mission Flexible Pool allocation of Rs.2285.00 crore in 2008-09 falls
short of the requirements envisaged under different components. Additional funds under the Mission
Flexible Pool will be provided during the course of the year.
Action Taken
For Bihar, funds under RCH Flexible Pool could not be released up to December 2008
owing to the unspent balance of Rs.112.63 crore already available as on 1.4.2007. It may be
mentioned that Bihar, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, Tamil Nadu and Goa were the low performing states
in utilisation of funds during 2007-08. It is agreed that there is no shortage of funds in the light
of the pace of utilization by some of the States. It is felt that some delay occurs in transmission
of funds from States to the districts and lower level owing to the non-availability of electronic
transfer facility. Secondly, the reporting of expenditure from the block/district levels to the State
and central level is also sometimes delayed owing to lengthy reporting track as a result of which
short reporting of utilization also occurs. These practical difficulties are being identified and sorted
out by the frequent trainings and workshops being organised by the financial management experts
of the Central Financial Management Group (FMG) in association with the State FMGs and the

85
pioneer institutes like NIHFW, RRCs and the SHSRCs. As regards the budget allocation of
Rs.2285 crores under the Mission Flexible Pool, the same is considered as short in comparison
with the requirements presented in the State Programme Implementation Plans for 2008-09.
However, the position would be reviewed at the RE stage on the basis of the trend of utilisation
of funds during the first 5-6 months of the current financial year.

COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE

that balance remaining at the end of the year is mainly due to curtailed releases owing to
86

87
low absorptive capacity in the NE region. The Committee is constrained to observe that the

Recommendations/Observations
2.6 A perusal of the funds allocation/utilization table referred to above reveals that there have
been instances of persistent under utilization of funds as well as steady decrease in allocation of
funds during the years 2006-07. 2007-08 and 2008-09 in case of various schemes like Grants to
States Training Institutions. Free Distribution of Contraceptives. Routine Immunization, Area
Projects, NGOs. Public Private Partnership and Integrated Diseases Surveillance Project. The
Committee is unable to understand this trend of steady decrease in funds allocation/utilization.
Whether it was due to the States’ incapacity to make optimum utilization or due to complicated
procedural formalities involved in release of the central assistance is unclear. The Committee.
therefore. recommends that the Department should get an analytical study conducted to find out
the factors leading to steady decrease in allocation of funds as well as the under utilization thereof
year after year. The Committee would like to be informed of the findings of the above study as
well the remedial measures proposed to be taken for optimum utilization of funds for the schemes
referred to above.
Action Taken
The utilisation status of the Schemes mentioned in the Para over the years 2006-07,
2007-08 and 2008-09 (BE) is at Annexure-II.
It can be seen from the Annexure that the utilization of funds under Integrated Diseases
Surveillance Project (IDSP) was low due to delay of submission of Project Implementation Plans
(PIPs) and MoUs by “Phase-Ill States”, less procurement of general lab equipment as the number
of labs was reduced, and non-release of grants to NIC for networking for want of UCs and SOEs.
Moreover, low utilisation under Area Projects is on account of non-achievement of stipulated
benchmarks under the Scheme. Under these schemes, according to available information, lower
expenditure was not due to procedural formalities.
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE
The Committee is disappointed to note that the Department has chosen to remain
silent on the reasons of steadily decreasing allocation of funds to such an important
healthcare programme like the Routine Immunisation Programme. The Committee is also
not convinced by the justification given by the Department with regard to Integrated
Diseases Surveillance Project. The Department has also, not indicated in its reply as to what
extent the number of labs was reduced resulting in less expenditure on the procurement of
equipments. Similarly, it is also not clear as to what is the exact number of units that were
planned to be linked by the NIC network. The Department has informed the Committee
that funds for networking could not be released to NIC since UCs and SOEs could not be
timely obtained from it. The Committee is aware that National Informatics Centre (NIC) is
a Government setup. Therefore, it wonders as to what could be the reasons for which there

88
were delays in submission of UCs and SOEs by the latter. The Committee, under the
circumstances, can only conclude that the Department’s attempts in the matter have been
lackadaisical, and as a result of which, achieving targeted landmarks in fully
operationalising critical initiatives like that of the IDSP have been put on the back foot.
The Committee observes that the Department should actively pursue the project of making
IDSP fully operational and should be extra vigilant towards achieving the targets set for
such critical health initiatives.
